## 3. What separates strong from loose (the empirical comparison)

I picked ten explanations that were clearly strong and ten that were complete but loose, read all twenty side by side, then measured a few things.

- **Strong (10):** PT139-S4-Q18 Flaw, PT142-S4-Q22 Flaw, PT145-S2-Q19 Paradox, PT154-S1-Q18 NA, PT153-S2-Q13 Weaken, PT146-S2-Q13 Strengthen, PT147-S1-Q17 Evaluate, PT152-S2-Q21 Principle-Conform, PT150-S3-Q15 Agree/Disagree, PT144-S3-Q17 Role.
- **Loose but complete (10), with the failure each shows:** PT127-S2-Q3 Weaken (two wrong answers get one line each: "This supports the argument's explanation"); PT126-S4-Q20 Weaken (generic summary notes, a Goal that restates the task); PT124-S3-Q10 Role (short, and "Haha. This answer is unthinkably weird"); PT151-S2-Q18 Strengthen (117 words total; the 29% trap gets one sentence); PT146-S2-Q8 Flaw (every wrong answer is "The argument never discusses X", correct note is one line); PT142-S4-Q5 Principle (mechanical, no reason why the trap tempts); PT155-S2-Q10 Paradox (fine idea, thin notes); PT158-S3-Q2 Strengthen (long, but misquotes a choice); PT158-S2-Q16 Paradox (long, but brings in outside facts); PT148-S1-Q20 Method (sound analysis, but calls the correct answer "so, so dumb").

Measured averages (strong vs loose):

| Measure | Strong | Loose |
|---|---|---|
| Total words | 773 | 394 |
| Words in the Evaluate step | 169 | 77 |
| Words in the Goal step | 66 | 43 |
| Goal names 3+ content words from the stimulus | 0.9 | 0.5 |
| Avg words per wrong-answer note | 87 | 45 |
| Wrong-answer notes under 20 words (verdict only) | 0.0 per question | 1.1 per question |
| Wrong-answer notes that run an explicit test (negate it, does it weaken, does it trigger the rule, why should we believe it) | 1.4 of 4 | 0.2 of 4 |
| Words in the correct-answer note | 143 | 60 |
| Examples, analogies or worked numbers | 2.0 | 0.5 |
| Stated generalizations ("whenever", "on X questions", "usually") | 1.6 | 0.3 |

What actually separates them, in order of how reliably it predicted my judgment:

1. **The Evaluate step argues with the argument.** Strong ones do real work before the choices: they build the objection ("Given that X, how could we argue not-Y?"), do the arithmetic (25% of $400 vs 13% of $1,000), or set the two populations side by side. Loose ones restate the stimulus and move on.
2. **The Goal names the answer, not the task.** Strong: "look for an answer saying the fish in the leafless trees are exposed to predators that hunt by sight." Loose: "Find the flaw that mistakes a response to danger for a cause", "Identify an answer choice that supports fairness." Strong Goals often give two acceptable shapes (a linking answer or a defender).
3. **Every wrong answer gets a reason tied to this question, and usually a test.** The loose group's signature is the verdict-only note: "This is irrelevant.", "That is unnecessary.", "The argument never discusses profits." Strong notes say what the answer claims, what was needed, and where they diverge, often by running the family's test on it (negate it; would it weaken; is the trigger established; does the conclusion match the rule's right side).
4. **The popular trap gets the most attention.** Strong explanations spend their longest wrong-answer note on the choice that drew 15-30% of students and say why it tempts. Loose ones treat it like every other wrong answer.
5. **The correct-answer note explains the fit, and often generalizes it.** Strong: "Another correct answer performing the same function could have said..." (PT146-S2-Q13); the karaoke/Jenga reason why A → C still works when you predicted A and B → C (PT139-S4-Q21). Loose: "This is the flaw."
6. **Fidelity.** Two loose items are long. Length did not save them: a misquoted answer choice, imported outside facts, or the author disowning the question destroys model value regardless of word count.

What did not separate them: word count by itself (PT152-S4-Q19 is 479 words and strong; PT158-S2-Q16 is 716 and loose), the choice-tag vocabulary, and the presence of jokes (several strong ones joke; it only hurts when the joke is at the question's or the answer's expense).

## 4. Rubric used for the shortlist

Gate (hard requirement): full Argument breakdown and all five choice notes. Every shortlisted candidate passed.

Scored 0 / 1 / 2:

| Criterion | 2 | 1 | 0 |
|---|---|---|---|
| **P** Prediction | Goal names the content or shape of the right answer before the choices (may give two acceptable shapes) | Goal names the task or admits it cannot predict | No prediction |
| **X** Precision | Names the exact gap, flaw or relationship in this argument's words | Uses the right label but stays generic | Label only, or wrong |
| **E** Eliminations | Every wrong answer gets a question-grounded reason; the popular trap gets the most attention | Most do; one or two verdict-only notes | Verdict-only notes |
| **T** Transfer | States the move in a form that works on the next question of this type (the test, the rule, the recurring trap) | Transfer is implicit | None |
| **C** Clarity | Plain, no padding, no pasted repetition | Some padding or pasted notes | Hard to follow |
| **L** Learner value | Teaches what a student needs to understand and repeat the move: why the trap tempts, what to do when stuck, a worked example or analogy, a second correct answer that would also work | Justifies the key well but does not teach the move | Only justifies the key |

Plus a **fidelity flag** (not scored; disqualifying if serious): `slip` factual or typo error, `tone` the author mocks the question or answer, `register` explicit-Standard PT157/158 prose, `transcription` damaged text.

Learner value is the criterion that most often separated a 10 from a 12. Many explanations justify the key correctly (1) but only the best ones also show the student the move they will reuse (2).

## 5. Step structure per family (DB, most common sequences)
## 6. Recurring phrasing conventions

- **Frames:** "Given that ... how could we (still) argue that ..." (Weaken, Strengthen, NA, Flaw); "Curious Fact ... Author's Explanation ... Alternate Explanation / Plausibility" (any causal argument); "Given that ... How can it be that ..." (Paradox); "If Evidence, then Conclusion" (Principle, SA).
- **Tests in choice notes:** negate it and ask if it weakens (NA); "fails to consider X" means ask whether X weakens, "presumes X" means ask whether X is necessary (Flaw); "argues X by Y: X must match the conclusion and Y the evidence" (Method, Flaw); a rule can only prove its right-hand side (Principle, SA); "can we support the Agree position and the Disagree position?" (Agree/Disagree); "Why should we believe this?" (Role, Main Conclusion).
- **Recurring trap names:** Too Strong (the most common NA and MBT tag), Out of Scope, No Impact, Unclear Impact, No Distinction (Paradox), Deepens Paradox, Wrong Flaw / Not an Objection (Flaw), Bad Conclusion / Evidence / Trigger Match (Principle, Parallel), Premise / Last-Claim Trap / Opposing Position (Main Conclusion), Unsupported Agree/Disagree Position (Agree/Disagree), Fake Opposite / Illegal Negation (NA), Reversal (SA). Tags come from the local markdown; the dump does not include them.
- **Rules stated as rules:** "Most X can only be concluded from a Most X premise"; "some / sometimes / can is almost always wrong on Strengthen, Weaken, Paradox, SA"; "the second speaker almost never denies the first speaker's evidence"; "about 85-90% of Main Conclusion questions put the conclusion in the first sentence or after a pivot"; "on EXCEPT, three of the working answers usually work one way and one works another".
- **Voice habits that should not be copied:** mocking the question or answer ("hot garbage", "so, so dumb", "unthinkably weird", "dumpster fire"), and the PT157/158 em-dash register.

# Parallel: 40 candidates (of 104 complete)


=================== PT151-S2-Q19 | diff 3 | key B | score 24.7 | features: Conditional Diagramming, Repetitive Answers
STIMULUS: Munroe was elected in a landslide. It is impossible for
Munroe to have been elected without both a fundamental
shift in the sentiments of the electorate and a well-run
campaign. Thus, one cannot avoid the conclusion that
there has been a fundamental shift in the sentiments of
the electorate.
STEM: Which one of the following arguments is most closely
parallel in its reasoning to the argument above?
(A) [4% | Bad Premise Match] The Park Street Cafe closed this year even though its customer base was satisfied. So, because its customer base was satisfied, the only conclusion one can draw is that the Park Street Cafe closed because it was facing strong competition.
(B) [77% | Correct] The Park Street Cafe closed this year. So we must conclude that the Park Street Cafe was facing strong competition, since it would not have closed unless it was true both that it was facing strong competition and that its customer base was unsatisfied.
(C) [3% | Bad Conclusion/Premise Match] No one can argue that the Park Street Cafe closed this year because its customer base was not satisfied. Even if its customer base was not satisfied, the Park Street Cafe would have closed only if it was facing strong competition.
(D) [9% | Bad Conclusion/Premise Match] The Park Street Cafe closed this year. There was no reason for it to remain open if it was facing strong competition and had an unsatisfied customer base. So one cannot rule out the possibility that it was both facing strong competition and had an unsatisfied customer base.
(E) [7% | Weak Conclusion/Evidence Match] The Park Street Cafe closed this year. In order to stay open, it needed a lack of competition and it needed a satisfied customer base. Because it had neither, the unavoidable conclusion is that the Park Street Cafe could not have stayed open this year.
--- BREAKDOWN
## Evidence
Monroe was elected.
```
Monroe elected  →  fundamental shift in electorate  AND  well-run campaign
```
## Conclusion
One can't avoid the conclusion that there's been a fundamental shift in the sentiments of the electorate.
## Evaluate
This is a valid argument since they give us a conditional (signified by the certainty of "impossible for X to happen without both Y and Z"). In other words, X requires Y and Z.
  X → Y and Z
  They tell us that the trigger happened (Munroe was elected).
  X.
  From that, we already know that both Y and Z necessarily follow. Their conclusion for some reason only chooses to mention one of those things (still valid).
  Y.
## Goal
So we know to replicate this we need two premises:
  1. conditional premise (probably with AND in outcome)
  2. factual premise that triggers the conditional premise
  The conclusion
  3. the outcome of the conditional (or part of it)
  It's not necessary for the correct answer to replicate the fact that the conditional had a "Y and Z" outcome, but it probably will, so I would use that as a filtering mechanism on a first pass.
  But the essence of the argument is just
  premise: here's a rule
  premise: here's a fact that triggers the rule.
  conclusion: here's (part of) the outcome of rule.
--- CHOICE NOTES
(A) We know from the indicator words where to find our conclusion, the very last claim.
"So, because [support], [conclusion]"

Are either of the first two claims the Conditional premise we're looking for?

Nope. No conditional premise, so no bueno.

(notice how this answer is trying to appeal to us by using similar wording to draw its conclusion: "the only conclusion one can draw" is a very similar sounding conclusion to the original's "one cannot avoid the conclusion". Sometimes the correct answer mimics wording from the original argument, but usually superficial similarities like same topic or same language is how they write a trap answer)
(B) We know from the indicator words where to find our conclusion.
"So we must conclude [conclusion], since [support]."

Do we have a conditional premise?

Yes, the final claim is a conditional premise, and it even has an AND in the outcome.

```
cafe closed  →  facing strong competition  AND  customer base unsatisfied
```

Do we have a factual premise telling us that the trigger of this rule has happened?

Yes, the first claim is a factual premise telling us that the Cafe closed.

Is the conclusion saying that part of our rule's outcome is true?

Yes, the conclusion is saying that "face strong competition" must be true (as well as "customer base unsatisfied", of course).
(C) Here is what we were looking for
premise: here's a rule
premise: here's a fact that triggers the rule.
conclusion: here's (part of) the outcome of rule.

Does this argument provide a conditional rule?
Yes, with "only if", in the final claim. "Only if" always indicates the Right side (necessary) condition.

```
cafe closed  →  facing strong competition
```

This rule doesn't have an AND in the outcome, so if we're guessing aggressively, we should check out now. If we want to go further than our second premise should be factually establishing that the trigger happened.

Do we have a premise that says "the cafe closed"?

Not really, but it's sort of an embedded truth in the first sentence. That, again, is a weak match, so we would bail unless we were desperate to make this work.

If we're being charitable, then we have this so far:
premise: here's a rule
closed → facing strong competition
premise: here's a fact that triggers the rule.
closed.
conclusion: here's (part of) the outcome of rule.

Our conclusion should then be "we must conclude that they were facing strong competition."

However, the first sentence here is the conclusion and it's definitely not saying that.
(D) If we're peeking at conclusions to see if they match before even bothering to read the answer choice, this one would stick out as hopeless.

Original conclusion: one can't avoid concluding X
This conclusion: one can't rule out possibility of X

Those are claims with completely different levels of strength. The original conclusion is certain of itself. This answer's conclusion is only saying something is possible.

That would be enough to disqualify it. It does have a conditional premise, but the AND is on the trigger, not the outcome. "If" signifies a Left side (suff) condition.

```
facing strong competition AND unsatisfied customer base  →  no reason for it to remain open
```

We could make it more like the original by contraposing, but then it would be an OR.


It's hard to see quickly, but it actually has a third premise, so if we saw that we could bail.

What's our conditional premise?

```
staying open  →  a lack of competition  AND  satisfied customer base
```

Instead, the factual premise contradicts the trigger. And then the bonus premise no one asked for says "there was both competition and an unsatisfied customer base".

2339	This argument boils down to whether a certain action (improving the apartments) is a Net Gain. The author is arguing that the owners should make the apartments, so she thinks this action will yield a net positive. And she is rebutting the owners, who think it will be a Net Loss (the cost of the improvements > the increase in rent that they could charge). The author ends up changing the math on their calculation by bringing in another factor that would be affected by improving the apartments: they would also raise the property values and revenue ceiling on other assets owned by the owners of the apartments.

5	STIMULUS: Studies have shown that those who take daily doses of vitamin C are less likely to contract colds than are those who do not. Thus, if a person contracts a cold, he or she probably does not take daily doses of vitamin C.
6	STEM: The questionable reasoning in the argument above most closely parallels that in which one of the following?
7	(A) [2% | Bad Conclusion Match] To be classified as a hurricane, a tropical storm in the Atlantic Ocean must have sustained wind speeds of over 74 miles (119 kilometers) per hour. So tropical storms with sustained wind speeds at about that level are not hurricanes.
8	(B) [1% | Bad Evidence Match] Everyone who ate the tuna salad prepared in the cafeteria has contracted botulism. So those who ate other dishes prepared in the cafeteria will probably also get this disease.
9	(C) [1% | Bad Conclusion Match] Laboratory rats given an extremely low-calorie diet live up to 20 percent longer than do those given a more ordinary diet. So most people could extend their life spans by eating a great deal less than they usually do.
10	(D) [1% | Bad Conclusion Match] People who train diligently to play poker can achieve a certain level of proficiency, so those players owe their success to hard work alone.
11	(E) [96% | Correct] Automobile engines that undergo regular oil changes are more likely to be problem free than are those that do not. So automobiles with engine problems probably have not had regular oil changes.

13	## Conclusion (thus)
14	If a person contracts a cold, he or she probably doesn't take daily doses of vitamin C.
15	## Evidence
16	Studies show those who take daily doses of vitamin C are less likely to contract colds than are those who do not.
17	## Evaluate
18	At first this might seem like pretty sound reasoning. Since taking vitamin C daily seems to make one less likely to get a cold, we might think that someone with a cold probably doesn't take daily vitamin C. If we don't understand the flaw, we can at least focus on the structure, and the structure may be enough to get us to the correct answer. Since the ideas of "vitmain C" and "contracting a cold" appear in both premise and conclusion, this argument lends itself to an algebraic representation.
19	```
20	Prem:
21	People who do X are less likely to have Y happen
22	Conc:
23	If Y happens to someone, then they probably don't do X
24	```
25	Why is this actually a flawed argument? It's because there might be way more people who take daily vitamin C than those who don't. So even though taking vitamin C makes you less likely to get a cold, there will still be a ton of people who take vitamin C with colds. Here's an analogy. Say there are 300 million or so natural born citizens in the U.S. and maybe 20 million naturalized citizens (i.e. they were born elsewhere but at some point moved to the U.S., passed the citizenship test, and got U.S. citizenship). Foreign-born citizens of the U.S. are usually more likely to know American history (because they had to learn it to pass the citizenship test) and usually more likely to know formal rules of English grammar (because they had to learn English mainly through classes, not through informal conversation). So we could probably say that natural born U.S. citizens are less likely to know American history or less likely to know formal rules of grammar. But does that mean if we find an American who happens to know history or grammar, that they are probably not a natural born citizen? Or to put it another way, does that mean that "most of the Americans who know U.S. history or formal rules of grammar are foreign-born?" No, it doesn't. Even though foreign-born Americans are more likely to know that stuff, they are vastly outnumbered in raw totals by the quantity of natural born citizens, so it's still the case that "most of the Americans who know U.S. history and rules of grammar are natural born".
26	## Goal
27	Anywayyyy, since that is a tricky statistical flaw to understand, we are probably best served by taking our algebraic recipe into the answers.
28	```
29	Prem:
30	People who do X are less likely to have Y happen
31	Conc:
32	If Y happens to someone, then they probably don't do X
33	```

54	People owe their success to X alone

187	## Main Conclusion (should = opinion)
188	A vacationer should choose an airline that has had an accident in the past 5 years.
189	## Conclusion (So)
190	If an airline has had no accident during the past 5 years, the chances that the airline will have a crash are increased.
191	## Evidence
192	The average airline has 1 accident every 5 years.
193	## Evaluate
194	Since there are two conclusions drawn, we're technically accountable for evaluating both reasoning moves.
195	Given that... the average airline has 1 accident every 5 years, Should we accept that... if an airline hasn't had an accident during the past 5 years, the chances they will have a crash are increased?
196	No, that's called the Gambler's Fallacy. If you're flipping a coin with your buddy, and it's heads, does that mean the next flip is more likely to be tails?
197	No, it's a 50/50 "coin flip" every time.
198	What if the last five flips were all Heads? Doesn't that make it more likely that the next flip is Tails?
199	No. The past has no causal influence on the probability of your next coin flip.
200	Another potential issue we could raise is the idea of "the average airline". Let's say we were trying to figure out how many accidents airlines have on average over a 5 year span. **Last 5 years** Jet Blue: 0 accidents Virgin: 0 accidents Spirit: 3 accidents Southwest: 1 accidents
201	Dividing those 4 accidents by 4 airlines we'd get that "the average airline as 1 accident every five years". Does that mean that we should avoid JetBlue or Virgin because they didn't have any accidents? Shouldn't we maybe trust them more when it comes to safety because they didn't have any accidents?
202	Okay, what about the 2nd reasoning move:
203	Given that... an airline that's had an accident in the last five years has a lower chance of having a crash, Should we accept that... a vacationer should choose such an airline?
204	Maybe there are other factors that go into how a vacationer should choose an airline. Sure, "risk of crash" is one. But price / convenience / in-flight amenities and such could also be relevant concerns.
205	## Goal
206	It's probably more likely they'll test the Gambler's Fallacy flaw involved in reaching the Intermediate Conclusion, since that error occurs farther upstream and keeps the 2nd move to the Main Conclusion from even being relevant to critique.
207	But let's look for an answer that either interprets probability or averages too literally, thinking that past data points somehow alter the probability of something occurring. (Or find an answer where someone is making a recommendation based on that option having one advantage, when it's possible that other options have different advantages that outweigh this option's stated advantage).
208	--- CHOICE NOTES
209	(A) While this is tempting because we're primed to look for the Gambler's Fallacy, and the "coin flip" is the classic example used to illustrate it.
210	
211	But this argument makes sense. It's astronomically unlikely that a fair coin would come up heads 100 times in a row, so while that wouldn't **100%** prove it's not a fair coin, it would certainly support a very reasonable suspicion that it's not a fair coin.
212	(B) This only mimics the move from Premise to Intermediate Conclusion (it has no 2nd conclusion), but as we suspected, that is the main error LSAT wants us to catch.
213	
214	This answer, like the original argument, interprets average rates too literally and thinks that past data points influence future probability.
215	
216	The idea of Gambler's Fallacy is that because the probability of tails is 1/2, if you get heads a few times in a row, you feel overdue for some tails. That's an incorrect understanding of probability, though, which balances out to 1/2 over an infinite time scale.
217	
218	In this argument the author is acting like an adult male that hasn't ascended to dominance over the past 10 years is now overdue to ascend to dominance.
219	
220	What do we mean by "interpreting probability too literally"? If I have a six-sided die, the probability of rolling a 4 is 1/6. Rolling a 4 is one of six possible outcomes, so it has a 1/6 probability.
221	
222	Does that mean that if I roll a die six times, I'm guaranteed to get exactly one 4?
223	
224	Of course not. But that's how the author is thinking about probability.

226	If the average rate of airline accidents is 1 every 5 years, then over any 5 year time span, an airline should have exactly 1 accident.
227	
228	If a baboon has a 1/10 probability of ascending to dominance, then over any 10 year time span, that baboon should have ascended to dominance exactly once.
229	(C) This argument is attempting to apply a probability statistic to an individual, but it's unclear whether the statistic can be properly applied to that individual.

231	If I say, "the chance that an NFL player is left-handed is 1 in 8. Thus, Marty, an NFL player, has a 1 in 8 chance of being left-handed", that would be a reasonable application of a statistic.

234	
235	If we accept that Marty qualifies as an average resident, then there is no flaw. If we think that Marty doesn't qualify as an average resident, then that's a different flaw from the original.
236	(D) This is a flawed argument, but it's not committing a Gambler's Fallacy, it's just doing an inappropriate comparison and basically failing to consider how weighted averages might ruin its conclusion.

244	But since the conclusion just says "employed adult", it includes part-time and full-time adults. We don't have any information about what part-time adults make.

266	(D) [4% | Bad Evidence Match Weak Conclusion Match] Studies show that people who do not exercise regularly have a shorter life expectancy than those who exercise regularly. To help increase their patients’ life expectancy, doctors should recommend regular exercise to their patients.

271	## Intermediate Conclusion (can conclude)

273	## Conclusion (Therefore)

275	## Evaluate
276	Since there are two conclusion indicators here, we should think about whether there are flaws occurring at each move.
277	Is there a flaw in going from "75% of strict vegetarians reached age 50 w/o serious heart disease", "thus avoiding meat increases one's chances of avoiding serious heart disease"?
278	We'll sure. First of all, how does the 75% compare to meat-eaters? This author seems to assume that some lower percentage of meat-eaters reach age 50 w/o serious heart disease. But what if it's also 75% (or even higher)?
279	So one flaw in drawing that first conclusion is simply an Incomplete Comparison. The author is acting like 75% is better than some similar statistic for meat-eaters, but she never provided that other statistic
280	However, even if we grant that meat-eaters have a worse stat (let's say only 65% of meat-eaters make it to age 50 without serious heart disease), does that make it fair to conclude that "avoiding meat increases your chance of avoiding serious heart disease"?
281	No, because that would just mean that avoiding meat is correlated with lower rates of heart disease. We wouldn't know for sure whether that lower rate is being caused by the vegetarianism or if it could be explained a different way (maybe people who eat vegetarian are more likely to get more exercise than meat-eaters, and it's the extra exercise that is actually the thing lowering the heart disease risk).
282	So this second flaw in drawing the 1st conclusion is the famous Causal flaw. Just because there's a correlation between X and Y doesn't mean we can assume that "doing X increases your chance of Y".
283	What about the 2nd conclusion, the one in the final sentence?
284	Is there a flaw in going from "avoiding meat increases one's chances of avoiding serious heart disease" to "if you want to reduce your risk of serious heart disease, then you should not eat meat"?

696	STEM: The flawed nature of the company president’s argument can most effectively be demonstrated by noting that, by parallel reasoning, we could conclude that
697	(A) [1% | Bad Conclusion Match] the president of Corbin Corporation has an office that is not in Corbin’s headquarters building, since almost all of the offices in Corbin’s headquarters building are small, whereas Corbin’s president has a large office
698	(B) [20% | Bad Conclusion Match] Donna has at least ten years of experience as a computer programmer, since almost every programmer who works for Coderight Software has at least ten years experience, and Donna will probably be hired as a programmer by Coderight
699	(C) [5% | Bad Conclusion Match] almost all of Acme’s employees oppose the pending merger with Barrington Corporation, since almost all of Acme’s employees are factory workers, and almost all of the factory workers at Acme oppose the merger
700	(D) [52% | Correct] Robinson will probably be appointed as president of Sifton University, since almost every one of Sifton’s previous presidents had a Ph.D., and Robinson has a Ph.D.
701	(E) [23% | Valid Word Trap: last ten years] the novel Safekeeping will probably earn a profit for its publisher, Peninsula Press, since almost every novel published by Peninsula over the last ten years has been profitable

704	Almost every really successful Almost all X's product in last 10 yrs was in past time launched by massive TV period used Y ad campaign. We're using a massive We're using Y TV ad campaign for for Thing A. Vegetaste Burger.
705	## Conclusion
706	Vegetaste Burger will Thing A will probably be very successful. prob be X
707	## Evaluate
708	We could call this argument a few different things. Past = Future Since massive ads have gone with successful products in the past, the massive ad we're doing now will probably go with a successful product. Correlation = Causation Since almost every successful product was preceded by a major TV ad launch, we can conclude that massive ad launch for Vegetaste will cause it to be successful. Necessary vs. Sufficient (with Most claims) Most successful products had a massive ad launch. VB has a massive ad launch. So, VS will probably be a successful product. The flaw, conversationally, could be "just because Y has usually been preceded by X, that doesn't mean that this X will probably be followed by a Y. There might be tons of times that ~Y is also preceded by X. Maybe most of the time X occurs, Y doesn't. But most of the time Y occurs, X does."

Premise 2: Thing A is Y. Conclusion: Thing A is probably X. All the answers are starting with the conclusion, so we'll get rid of them if they're not matching the strength of probably (typically, generally, usually, likely, most, tends to). If the strength of language is correct, and the conclusion is saying 'probably X', then we'll look for a premise that talks about 'Almost every X' or 'Most X'.

733	=================== PT146-S3-Q25 | diff 4 | key D | score 19.4 | features: Math-y Stuff, Quantifiers
734	STIMULUS: At Morris University this semester, most of the sociology majors are taking Introduction to Social Psychology, but most of the psychology majors are not. Hence, there must be more sociology majors than psychology majors enrolled in the class.
735	STEM: The flawed pattern of reasoning in the argument above is most similar to that in which one of the following?
736	(A) [4% | Bad Conclusion Match] Most of the paintings on display at the Metro Art Museum are from the twentieth century, but most of the paintings the Metro Art Museum owns are from the nineteenth century. It follows that the museum owns few if any of the twentieth-century paintings it displays.
737	(B) [4% | Bad Premise / Conclusion Match] In an opinion poll of Silver Falls residents, more said they were in favor of increased spending on roads than said they were in favor of increased spending on parks. So most Silver Falls residents must be in favor of spending more on roads but opposed to spending more on parks.
738	(C) [11% | Bad Premise Match] In the San Felipe city arboretum, most of the trees are of local rather than exotic species. Therefore, in the San Felipe area, there must be more trees of local species than of exotic species.
739	(D) [64% | Correct] Most of the vegetables available at the Valley Food Co-op are organic, but most of the vegetables available at the Jumbo Supermarket are not. Thus, more organic vegetables are available at Valley Food than are available at Jumbo.
740	(E) [16% | Weaker Match] The Acme Realty website has photos of most of the houses, but of fewer than half of the condominiums, that Acme is offering for sale. So Acme must have more houses than condominiums for sale.
741	--- BREAKDOWN
742	## Conclusion
743	There must be more sociology majors than psychology majors enrolled in Intro to Social Psychology.
744	## Evidence
745	Most sociology majors are taking it. Most PSY majors are not taking it.
746	## Evaluate
747	Because this argument has conditional/quantified terms, we could potentially get it right without understanding the flaw, as long as we convert it into a symbolic recipe. Most A's are in place X. Most B's are not in place X. Thus, there are more A's than B's in place X. The flaw revolves around the fact that we're making two Most claims, but they're about different groups. You can't really compare such claims without knowing about the relative size of those different groups. For example, let's say at Morris University that there 100 sociology majors and 1000 psychology majors. Most sociology majors are in this class, so at least 51 sociology majors are there. Most psychology majors are not in this class, so at most 499 psychology majors are there. Have I convinced you that this class has more sociology majors than psychology majors? Of course not. There might be way more psychology majors present, if the group of all psychology majors is bigger than the group of all sociology majors.
748	## Goal
749	Look for two Most premises, and a conclusion saying one number is bigger than another. Look for something resembling this recipe: Most A's are in place X. Most B's are not in place X. Thus, more A's than B's in place X. Or look for an argument similarly vulnerable to the objection of, "What if one of those groups is much bigger than the other?"
750	--- CHOICE NOTES
751	(A) We do have two Most premises, but the conclusion isn't saying that one quantity is bigger than another, so there's no reason to dig deeper here.
752	(B) There's only one premise, so that's an immediate deal breaker. The conclusion also isn't saying that one quantity is bigger than another (it's saying, "most people prefer one thing over another").
753	(C) There's only one premise, so that's an immediate deal breaker. The conclusion, to its credit, is lovingly saying that one quantity is bigger than another.
754	(D) From a bird's eye view, it's got two Most premises and a conclusion that compares quantities. Here's how it would match with the original argument. Most SOC are taking Intro to SP / Most available veggies at VCF are organic. Most PSY are not taking Intro to SP / Most available veggies at JS are not organic. So, more SOC than PSY are taking Intro to SP / More VFC veggies than JS veggies are organic. Both arguments are vulnerable to the objection, "What if one of those groups is much bigger?" If Jumbo Supermarket sells 1000 vegetables, but Valley Co-op sells 100, Valley Co-op sells a minimum of 51 organic. Jumbo sells a maximum of 499 organic. So there might be more organic vegetables available at Jumbo.
755	(E) This one is very close. There are two Most premises (if we hear the first two claims as "most houses for sale have a website photo" and "most condos for sale do not have a website photo"), and the conclusion is indeed a quantitative comparison. And this is also vulnerable to the "what if one group is much bigger" objection. If there are 1000 condos for sale, and only 100 houses for sale, then at least 51 of the houses would have photos on the site and at most 499 condos would have photos on the site. A matching conclusion, though, would have said, "So there must be more photos of houses on the website than there are photos of condominiums." This conclusion went one level farther by talking about the total number of houses / condos for sale. This argument is also vulnerable to a different kind of objection, one that isn't mathematical in nature, but Causal: Hey, author, maybe there aren't fewer condo photos on the website because there are fewer condos for sale but just because website pictures of condos aren't as sexy as website pictures of houses.

776	  S supports and H supports → approved

784	  Or, more loosely, look for some sort of He Said / She Said type argument, where the author arbitrarily chooses one side over the other. Both S and H claimed they supported the proposal. We know that at least one of them didn't, but how is the author capriciously choosing H as the guilty party? Couldn't it have been S?

869	STIMULUS: Of the citizens who disapprove of the prime minister’s
870	overall job performance, most disapprove because of the
871	prime minister’s support for increasing the income tax.

873	be increased. So Theresa probably approves of the prime
874	minister’s overall job performance.

876	reasoning that is most parallel to that in the argument
877	above?
878	(A) [67% | Correct] Of the people who support allowing limited logging in the Grizzly National Forest, most support it because they think it will reduce the risk of fire in the forest. Andy thinks that limited logging will not reduce the risk of fire in the forest, so he probably opposes allowing limited logging there.
879	(B) [12% | Bad Premise Match] Of the people who expect the population in the area to increase over the next ten years, most think that an expected population increase is a good reason to build a new school. Bonita does not expect the population to increase over the next ten years, so she probably does not favor building a new school.
880	(C) [8% | Bad Premise Match] Of the people who believe that the overall economy has improved, most believe it because they believe that their own financial situation has improved. Chung believes that the economy has worsened, so he probably believes that his own financial situation has worsened.
881	(D) [10% | Bad Premise Match] Of the people who oppose funding a study to determine the feasibility of building a light rail line in the Loffoch Valley, most also believe that the Valley Freeway should be built. Donna opposes increasing funding for a study, so she probably supports building the Valley Freeway.
882	(E) [4% | Bad Premise Match] Of the people who believe that there will be a blizzard tomorrow, most believe it because of the weather report on the Channel 9 news. Eduardo believes that there will be a blizzard tomorrow, so he probably saw the weather report on the Channel 9 news.
883	--- BREAKDOWN
884	## Conclusion (So)
885	Theresa probably approves of the prime minister's overall job performance.
886	## Evidence
887	Most people who disapprove of the PM's performance do so because of the PM's support for increasing the income tax.
888	But, Theresa believes that the income tax should be increased.
889	## Evaluate
890	Because of the symbol repetition in this argument (theresa / income tax / prime minister are all mentioned twice) and because of the quantifier most, this argument seems like a good contender for turning into an algebraic recipe.
891	```
892	Prem 1: Most people who are X are Y
893	        (X = disapprove of PM, Y = don't want income tax increased)
894	Prem 2: Person A is not Y.
895	        (A = Theresa)
896	Conc:   Person A is probably not X.
897	```
898	Conversationally, we could say that "even though Theresa supports the increased tax, that doesn't mean she probably supports the PM". We don't know whether most people who support the tax support the PM .
899	There could be 100 people, 98 of whom oppose the PM.
900	Most of those 98 oppose the PM because of the increased tax.
901	So let's say 60 of the 98 oppose the PM because of the increased tax.
902	That leaves 38 people who oppose the PM, but not because of the tax.
903	And 2 people who support the PM.
904	If we know that Theresa is not opposed to the tax, does that mean she's more likely to be one of the 2 people who support the PM or the 38 people who oppose the PM for some reason other than the tax?
905	Numerically, the latter is more probable.
906	## Goal
907	The conversational objection is harder to deal with, so we should probably look for our algebraic structure:
908	```
909	P1: Most people who are X are Y.
910	P2: Person A is not Y.
911	C: Person A is probably not X.
912	```
913	--- CHOICE NOTES
914	(A) This matches our structure really well.
915	
916	```
917	P1: Most people who are X are Y.
918	    Most ppl who support limited logging are thinking it'll reduce the risk of fire in the forest.
919	
920	P2: Person A is not Y.
921	    Andy does not link that limited logging will reduce the risk of fire.
922	
923	C: Person A is probably not X.
924	   Andy probably doesn't support limited logging.
925	```
926	(B) This is pretty close.
927	
928	```

We can also focus on the type and strength of claims used. The premises should involve a comparison between two categories of things. There should be a second premise about one of those categories of things. The conclusion should be an all claim. But we can also choose to respond conversationally. Microscopic organisms don't have a nervous system or a brain or, probably, any consciousness at all. How could they feel pain? 2. Why is the conclusion talking about all microscopic organisms? 1508	STIMULUS: Most kinds of soil contain clay, and virtually every kind of soil contains either sand or organic material, or both. Therefore, there must be some kinds of soil that contain both clay and sand and some that contain both clay and organic material.
1509	STEM: The pattern of flawed reasoning in which one of the following arguments is most parallel to that in the argument above?
1510	(A) [14% | Bad Conclusion Match] Most pharmacies sell cosmetics. Virtually every pharmacy sells shampoo or toothpaste, or both. Therefore, if there are pharmacies that sell both cosmetics and toothpaste, there must also be some that sell both cosmetics and shampoo.
1511	(B) [8% | Bad Conclusion Match] Undoubtedly, most pharmacies sell cosmetics, for almost all pharmacies sell either shampoo or toothpaste, or both, and there are some pharmacies that sell both cosmetics and shampoo and some that sell both cosmetics and toothpaste.
1512	(C) [4% | Bad Conclusion Match] Most pharmacies sell cosmetics. Nearly all pharmacies sell shampoo or toothpaste, or both. Therefore, unless there are some pharmacies that sell both cosmetics and toothpaste, there must be some that sell both cosmetics and shampoo.
1513	(D) [17% | Weak Premise Match] Virtually every pharmacy sells shampoo also sells toothpaste. Most pharmacies sell cosmetics. Therefore, there must be some pharmacies that sell both cosmetics and toothpaste and some that sell both cosmetics and shampoo.
1514	(E) [57% | Correct] Nearly all pharmacies sell either shampoo or toothpaste, or both. Therefore, since most pharmacies sell cosmetics, there must be some pharmacies that sell both cosmetics and toothpaste and some that sell both cosmetics and shampoo.
1515	--- BREAKDOWN
1516	## Evidence
1517	```
1518	Most soil has clay              Most A are B
1519	Most soil has sand              Most A are C or D
1520	or organic material
1521	```
1522	## Conclusion
1523	```
1524	Some soil is clay and sand      Some A are C
1525	and                             and
1526	Some soil is clay and org       Some A are D
1527	```
1528	## Evaluate
1529	Because we see “most” in one premise and “some” in the conclusion, we want to think of “virtually every” (i.e. “almost all”) as providing us another “most” claim.
1530	  Why? Well, this argument comes close to making the famous and legal “most + most” inference, which looks like this:
1531	```
1532	Most A’s are B
1533	+
1534	Most A’s are C
1535	--------------------
1536	Some B are C
1537	```
1538	  With the ingredients we were provided here, we could have made this inference:
1539	```
1540	Most S’s have C
1541	+
1542	Most S’s have SD or OM
1543	-------------------------------
1544	Some C are (SD or OM)
1545	```
1546	  What the author is doing illegally is thinking he can break that up into two different takeaways: Some C are SD, and Some C are OM.
1547	  Conversationally, the problem with the conclusion is that it’s possible that sand never overlaps with clay, or that organic material never overlaps with clay. It’s not possible that both of them never overlap. At least one of them does need to overlap with clay, but both of them don’t have to.
1548	  If you crave numbers for seeing this flaw, let’s say there are 11 types of soil.
1549	  6 clay, 5 no clay (most soil has clay)
1550	  8 sand, 2 org material, 1 neither (almost all has sand or org material)
1551	  Does there have to be soil that has clay and sand? Yes. 6 + 8 add up to 14, but there are only 11 types of soil, so we know that 3 of them overlap.
1552	  Does there have to be soil that has clay and organic material? No. It’s very possible that the 2 types of soil that have organic material are 2 of the 5 types of soil that don’t have clay.
1553	## Goal
1554	The cool thing about Parallel Flaw is that we don’t have to thoroughly understand the original argument’s flaw in order to match the argument, when the original argument has conditional or quantified logic.
1555	  We need two Most premises, and one of them needs to have an “or” outcome. The conclusion needs to make two Some claims, one for each member of that “or” outcome.
1556	```
1557	Premise 1: Most A’s are B
1558	Premise 2: Most A’s are C or D
1559	Conclusion: Some A’s are C, and Some A’s are D
1560	```

1562	(A) This conclusion is conditional. We want a conclusion that makes two Some claims. The premises did match:
1563	
1564	Most pharmacies sell cosmetics.
1565	Most pharmacies sell shampoo or toothpaste.
1566	
1567	But the conclusion would have needed to be “Some places that sell cosmetics also sell shampoo, and some places that sell cosmetics also sell toothpaste”.
1568	(B) This conclusion is a Most claim. We want a conclusion that makes two Some claims. The words “for, after all, since, because” (F.A.B.S.) directly indicate premises and indirectly indicate conclusions. 1572	Most pharmacies sell shampoo or toothpaste.
1573	
1574	But the conclusion would have needed to be “Some places that sell cosmetics also sell shampoo, and some places that sell cosmetics also sell toothpaste”.

1580	Most sandy soil has organic material.

1670	(D) [86% | Correct] The flooding in the basement caused damage to the furnace and also caused a short in the electrical system. Therefore, we cannot claim that the flooding in the basement caused the fire.
1671	(E) [1% | Not a Flaw / Bad Premise Match / Bad Conclusion Match] We have good reason to believe that the cause of the flooding along the coast was the unusually high tides. 1673	## Conclusion (hence)
1674	We can’t say that the asteroid impact caused the T’s extinction.
1675	## Evidence
1676	The asteroid impact caused a huge fire, and it caused climate change. We can’t prove that the fire caused T’s extinction, nor can we prove that climate change caused T’s extinction.
1677	## Evaluation
1678	This is probably one where many students aren’t sure exactly what to call the flaw, but they could still think about the structure of the argument and make eliminations based on mismatches in the answers. This argument is flawed because you can sometimes be sure that something had a certain effect, even if you’re unclear on the exact specifics of how. Say that someone dies in a helicopter crash. You might not know whether they died on impact or died from the resulting fire, but you still know that the helicopter crash killed them. Similarly, we might know that the asteroid impact killed off the T’s, even if we don’t know whether it was the fire or the climate change that ultimately did them in (maybe it was a combination of both). This is a subtle variation of part vs. whole. We can’t say that either part of the asteroid’s impact killed the T’s, but maybe we can still say that asteroid impact as a whole killed the T’s.

But tire pressure will become too low if the car owner neglects to check the pressure regularly. Therefore, if the car owner neglects to check the pressure regularly, a tire will wear out prematurely.
1816	(E) [2% | Bad Premise Match] If a tire's pressure is too low, the tire will wear out prematurely. But it will also wear out prematurely if it is often driven on gravel roads. Therefore, if a tire is often driven on gravel roads, keeping its pressure from becoming too low will not help it to last longer.
1817	--- BREAKDOWN

1819	If a bird feeder doesn't have protective cover, a bird feeder will not attract many birds.
1820	## Evidence
1821	If a bird feeder has a protective cover, then squirrels won't eat from the feeder. If squirrels eat from the feeder, it won't attract many birds.
1822	## Evaluate
1823	Since this has three conditional logic claims (if / only if / if) and has ideas that show up multiple times (squirrels eat / attract birds / protective cover), we would probably be wise to diagram this symbolically. And since we're doing Flaw / Parallel Flaw and we're seeing conditional logic, we can already be 90% sure that the flaw in the argument is an illegal negation or reversal (i.e. the Necessary vs. Sufficient flaw). The author is trying to put together two conditionals to arrive at her conclusion. Since "squirrels eating" is the common term in the two conditional premises, it's supposed to be the middle link in the chain. The author thinks she's combining these two premises: No cover → Squirrels eat Squirrels eat → Not many birds If those were actually the two premises, then she would correctly be deriving her conclusion: No cover -—--—--—--—> Not many birds The problem is the second premise says, Squirrels eat → No cover The word "only if" is the opposite of "if". When we see "if", the idea that goes with that word ends up on the left side of the arrow (the sufficient condition). When we see "only if", the idea that goes with that ends up on the right side (sufficient condition).

1863	=================== PT122-S4-Q24 | diff 4 | key A | score 17.0 | features: Famous Flaws, Quantifiers, Repetitive Answers
1864	STIMULUS: Most of the employees of the Compujack Corporation are computer programmers. Since most computer programmers receive excellent salaries from their employers, at least one Compujack employee must receive an excellent salary from Compujack.
1865	STEM: Which one of the following arguments exhibits a flawed pattern of reasoning most similar to the flawed pattern of reasoning exhibited by the argument above?
1866	(A) [58% | Correct] Most gardeners are people with a great deal of patience. Since most of Molly's classmates are gardeners, at least one of Molly's classmates must be a person with a great deal of patience.
1867	(B) [20% | Weak Conclusion Match] Most of Molly's classmates are gardeners. Since most gardeners are people with a great deal of patience, some of Molly's classmates could be people with a great deal of patience.
1868	(C) [12% | Weak Conclusion Match] Most gardeners are people with a great deal of patience. Since most of Molly's classmates are gardeners, at least one of Molly's classmates who is a gardener must be a person with a great deal of patience.
1869	(D) [5% | Bad Premise Match] Most gardeners are people with a great deal of patience. Since most of Molly's classmates who garden are women, at least one female classmate of Molly's must be a person with a great deal of patience.
1870	(E) [5% | Bad Premise Match / Valid Logic] Most of Molly's classmates are gardeners with a great deal of patience. Since most of Molly's classmates are women, at least one female classmate of Molly's must be a gardener with a great deal of patience.
1871	--- BREAKDOWN
1872	## Evidence
1873	Most Compujack employees Most A's are B's. are computer programmers.
1874	Most computer programmers Most B's are C's. receive excellent salaries.
1875	## Conclusion
1876	There must be at least one Some A's are C's. Compujack employee that receives an excellent salary.
1877	## Evaluate
1878	The cool thing with Parallel Flaw is that if there are obvious Quantifier / Conditional keywords, then we can potentially get the correct answer just by matching the structural signposts, regardless of whether we actually understand how the original argument was flawed.
1879	This is one of those cases. The two premises are Most claims. The conclusion is "at least one", which is equivalent to Some.
1880	The relationship between the two premises we need to replicate is that one Most claim begins where the other Most claim ends (the overlapping idea computer programmers is the end of one Most claim and the beginning of another).
1881	If you're curious why this is flawed reasoning, we're only allowed to prove a Trait Overlap when the two most facts are about the same group.
1882	A valid argument would sound like this: Most employees at Compujack Corporation are programmers. Most employees at Compujack Corporation have excellent salaries. Thus, at least one programmer has excellent salary.
1883	The valid "Most + Most" form looks like this:
1884	**Most A's** are B **Most A's** are C
1885	infer: B and C have at least some overlap
1886	The invalid form here is this:
1887	**Most A's** are B **Most B's** are C
1888	infer: A and C have at least some overlap
1889	This is not good logic as you can see from this argument: Most death row inmates are adult men. Most adult men have full-time jobs. Thus, at least one death row inmate has a full-time job.
1890	## Goal
1891	We want this form: **Most A's** are B **Most B's** are C infer: A and C have at least **some** overlap
1892	The quickest things to scan for would be making sure we have two Most premises and one Some conclusion. And the two Most premises should "chain" together (i.e. one begins where the other ends).
1893	--- CHOICE NOTES
1894	(A) -Most of Molly's mates are gardener's Most A's are B -Most gardener's are people w/ patience Most B's are C -So, some Molly's mates are Some A's are C people w/ patience
1895	
1896	As is often the case, the chronological order of ingredients is scrambled. The "since X, Y" form tells us that Y is the conclusion. We should be proactively looking to arrange the two Most claims so that the first one ends where the second begins.
1897	(B) This one is very close (almost identical to A), but the conclusion doesn't have the same strength as the original argument.
1898	
1899	Original conclusion: at least one C-employee **must** receive great salary.
1900	
1901	This conclusion: at least one Molly's mates **could be** people w/ patience.
1902	
1903	This isn't a flawed argument, because, sure, it's possible that there's some Trait Overlap. Our only objection to the original argument is that there isn't mandatory overlap.
1904	(C) Most of Molly's Most A's are B mates are gardener's
1905	
1906	Most gardener's are Most B's are C people w/ patience Looks good so far, but this should be the conclusion. So, some Molly's mates are Some A's are C people w/ patience
1907	
1908	Instead, we get a more complicated conclusion.
1909	
1910	So, some Molly's mates who Some A's who are are gardener's are people w/ patience B are C
1911	
1912	This is flawed in the same sense as the original, because the conclusion thinks there **must** be a Trait Overlap, when there does not have to be.
1913	
1914	But the structure of the conclusion is different, so (A) is the most similar pattern.
1915	(D) We were looking for two most claims, one of which begins the way the other ends: Most A's are **B's**. Most **B's** are C.
1916	
1917	Here, the premises do not have that match.
1918	
1919	Most gardener's are **people w/ great deal of patience**. Most of **Molly's mates who garden** are women.
1920	(E) We were looking for two most claims, one of which begins the way the other ends: Most A's are **B's**. Most **B's** are C.
1921	
1922	Here, the premises do not have that match. They both start with the same group.
1923	
1924	Most of Molly's mates are **women.** Most of **Molly's mates** are gardener's w/ patience.
1925	
1926	In fact, this answer is valid logic. Since both Most claims are about the same group (Molly's classmates) there must be at least some overlap between the two ending ideas (gardener with patience / woman).

1929	=================== PT153-S2-Q25 | diff 4 | key B | score 17.0 | features: Belief / Knowledge / Intent
1930	STIMULUS: Katelin says that we will be hit by a major snowstorm tomorrow. So she probably believes that tomorrow’s antique car show will be canceled, for it will certainly not be held if we are hit by a major snowstorm.
1931	STEM: The questionable pattern of reasoning in the argument above is most closely parallel to that in which one of the following?
1932	(A) [12% | Weak Premise Match] Jorge says that given today’s pollution levels, many species of migratory birds will become extinct. Thus, he probably believes that the extinctions will occur, since it is widely known that pollution might not be reduced at all.
1933	(B) [65% | Correct] Bo says that the soil in his backyard is poorly drained, and since raspberry bushes will not grow well in soil that is poorly drained, Bo probably believes that raspberry bushes will not grow well in his backyard.
1934	(C) [7% | Weak Premise Match / Valid Logic] Wanda says that no form of coercive force is ever justified. Thus, since most people who say this believe that government should be abolished, Wanda probably believes that government should be abolished.
1935	(D) [10% | Bad Conclusion/Premise Match] My chemistry professor says that most chemists are good at math. So, my chemistry professor might believe that most chemists are good at chess, since most people who are good at math are also good at chess.
1936	(E) [6% | Bad Premise Match] Dr. Bowder says that eating garlic increases one’s alertness. So, since she knows that whatever improves one’s circulation increases one’s alertness, Dr. Bowder probably believes that eating garlic increases one’s circulation.
1937	--- BREAKDOWN
1938	## Evidence
1939	```
1940	Katelin says we'll be       Thing A predicts X.
1941	hit by a major
1942	snowstorm tomorrow.
1943	If we are hit be a major    If X happens,
1944	snowstorm, then the         Y happens.
1945	antique car show will
1946	be canceled.
1947	```
1948	## Conclusion
1949	```
1950	Katelin must believe        Thing A believes
1951	the antique car show        Y will happen.
1952	will be canceled
1953	```
1954	## Evaluate
1955	This is a flaw that has been used a handful of times throughout LSAT's history. It's assuming that someone believes an Implication of their Belief, which is flawed because we don't know whether the person has enough information to connect one belief to its implication (or whether they have the will/skill to derive inferential beliefs, if they do have that information). For example: Katelin says that Clark Kent will be late to this morning's meeting. Clark Kent is Superman. So, Katelin probably believes that Superman will be late to this morning's meeting. Maybe you and I know that Clark Kent is Superman, but we don't know if Katelin does. If she doesn't know "Clark Kent is Superman", then there'd be no reason for her to have any belief about Superman being late to the meeting. Similarly, if she has no idea that "a major snowstorm would effectively cancel the antique car show" (she might not even be aware there is an antique car show planned), then she might not have any beliefs about tomorrow's antique car show.
1956	## Goal
1957	Look for an example where one Premise says "Person A believes X". A second premise should convey, "if X, then Y". And then the Conclusion should be saying, "so Person A probably believes Y."
1958	--- CHOICE NOTES
1959	(A) We do have someone with a belief:
1960	Jorge believes many migratory birds will become extinct.
1961	
1962	Is there an implication stated of what happens if migratory birds become extinct?
1963	
1964	No, the other premise doesn't even talk about birds. It just says "pollution might not be reduced at all".
1965	(B) We do have someone with a belief:
1966	Bo believes his backyard soil is poorly drained.
1967	
1968	Is there an implication stated of what happens if soil is poorly drained?
1969	
1970	Yes! If soil is poorly drained, then raspberry bushes won't grow well.
1971	
1972	So does the conclusion accuse Bo of believing that "raspberry bushes won't grow well"?
1973	
1974	Yes, and it correctly matches the strength of the original conclusion (probably).
1975	(C) We do have someone with a belief:
1976	W believes no coercive force is ever justified.
1977	
1978	Is there an implication stated of what happens if it's never justified to use coercive force?
1979	
1980	Not quite. The other premise doesn't say that something else is implied by this belief; it just says that most people who have this first belief also have this 2nd belief.
1981	
1982	So concluding that W probably also has this 2nd belief is actually a valid probabilistic conclusion, since "most" people who believe what she believes hold that 2nd belief too.
1983	(D) The quickest way to get rid of this answer is to peek at the conclusion and see that it's saying "my chemistry professor might believe". That's different from concluding "probably believes".
1984	
1985	Concluding that someone might believe something is incredibly safe, usually valid. It would only be invalid if we had established that it would be impossible for someone to believe something.
1986	
1987	So we're not going to replicate the flaw with "might".
1988	
1989	If we happened to dig in deeper, we do have someone with a belief:
1990	chemistry professor believes that most chemists are good at math.
1991	
1992	Is there an implication stated of what happens if most chemists are good at math?
1993	
1994	Not really. There's no conditional implication. There's some chance that the chemist who's good at math is also good at chess, but the two things don't need to overlap.
1995	(E) We do have someone with a belief:
1996	Dr. Bowder believes eating garlic increases one's alertness.
1997	
1998	Is there an implication stated of what happens if garlic increases alertness?
1999	
2000	No, there's just a premise saying that "something else also increases alertness".
2001	
2002	2 problems:
2003	- we wouldn't get any implication by combining "eat garlic → more alert" and "improve circulation → more alert", because those two don't chain together.
2004	
2005	- this second premise is identified as something Dr. Bowder knows. In order to mimic the original argument, the second premise should be a conditional relationship, but we aren't supposed to know whether or not the person we're talking about is aware of that relationship.
2006	
2007	This answer choice is acting more like, "Katelin thinks Clark Kent will be late. And, since Katelin knows that Clark Kent is Superman, .... "
2008

2071	(A) [6% | Not Quite Circular] Greeting one's coworkers must be a polite thing to do, because people who are considered polite always greet their coworkers. The proof that these people really are polite is that they are consistently polite in their daily lives.
2072	(B) [83% | Correct] This card game must be intellectually challenging, because it is played by highly intelligent people, who play only intellectually challenging card games. In fact, these players' intelligence is demonstrated by the fact that they play this game.
2073	(C) [3% | Missing the Circular] When coffee is being chosen, Brand Z is the coffee chosen by people with highly developed taste in coffee. These people showed their highly developed taste in coffee by correctly distinguishing eight brands of coffee from each other in a taste test.
2074	(D) [3% | Missing the Circular] That jacket must have been made for a very short person, because only very short people were able to fit into it. We know that they were very short because we saw them before they tried on the jacket.
2075	(E) [5% | Missing the Circular] This painting is a poor imitation, because only people with poor eyesight mistook it for the original. That these people have poor eyesight is demonstrated by the fact that they also mistook a vase of flowers in the painting for a peacock.

2192	(B) [57% | Correct] At a certain college, either philosophy or engineering is the most demanding major. Therefore, either the introductory course in philosophy or the introductory course in engineering is the most demanding introductorylevel course at that college.
2193	(C) [21% | Part vs. Part] For many years its superior engineering has enabled the Lawson Automobile Company to make the best racing cars. Therefore, its passenger cars, which use many of the same parts, are unmatched by those of any other company.
2194	(D) [2% | Part vs. Part] Domestic cats are closely related to tigers. Therefore, even though they are far smaller than tigers, their eating habits are almost the same as those of tigers.
2195	(E) [3% | Better vs. Best (Wrong Flaw) / Topic Trap] If a suit of questionable merit is brought in the first district rather than the second district, its chances of being immediately thrown out are greater. 2196	--- BREAKDOWN
2197	## Conclusion
2198	Our highest court is the least fair of any court on the continent and ought not to be emulated.
2199	## Evidence
2200	Our country has the least fair court system of any country on the continent and ought not to be emulated.
2201	## Evaluate
2202	The evidence and conclusion both deal with the idea of "least fair of any / shouldn't be emulated". They are only different because the evidence is saying this idea is true about the court system, while the conclusion is saying this idea is true about the highest court.
2203	If something is true about the court system, does that mean it's true of the highest court?
2204	Not necessarily. This is a famous flaw called **Part vs. Whole**. Maybe the average turnaround time for a case in the court system is 1 month, but the average turnaround time for a case in the highest court is 3 months. The Whole and its Parts do not necessarily have identical traits. Maybe this country's court system overall is the worst, but their highest court is actually pretty decent. It would be like a basketball team who is one of the worst in the league and ought not to be emulated, but their best player might still be good and worthy of emulation.
2205	## Goal
2206	Look for an answer that also makes this illegal move: "Because The Whole has trait X, Some Part of the whole must have trait X"
2207	--- CHOICE NOTES

2209	
2210	Instead, the conclusion introduces some complications -- Maureen was born in a mid-size town, but who knows if she currently lives there. Monica is now a resident of a mid-size town, but only recently.
2211	(B) This argument makes the same move from "if it's true of the Whole, then it's true of the Part".
2212	
2213	Either philosophy or engineering is the most demanding major (a whole). Thus, either Intro to Phil or Intro to English is the most demanding intro course (a part).
2214	
2215	This answer is hoping to strike people as unappealing since it introduces the structural mismatch of the either/or. But this answer is the only one that gives us a Whole to Part flaw. 2216	(C) This is making a Comparison flaw, assuming that "because trait X is true of Thing 1, trait X should also be true of similar Thing 2."
2217

2219	
2220	Passenger cars are not a part of racing cars. They are both Parts of the Whole of Lawson Auto Company.
2221

2224	(E) This compares the first district to the second district, which is not the Whole vs. Part relationship we're looking for. 2241	Parallel Flaw usually gives us argument with a lot of conditionals or quantifiers (which we can diagram), or they give us one of the Top 6 Famous Flaws:
2242	  - Necessary vs. Sufficient

2244	  - Ad Hominem
2245	  - Unproven vs. Proven False
2246	  - Causal
2247	  - Sampling
2248	  - This argument could technically be categorized as either of the last two. The Causal flaw is when an author overconfidently assumes one possible explanation for a curious fact, when other possible explanations could exist.
2249	  - Here, our author is assuming that using VIVVY was an important causal factor in these three kids becoming successful university students. But how do we know if VIVVY made any difference? Maybe they also had a lot of after school tutoring. Maybe they studied really hard. The author is taking this one causal possibility and acting like it definitely happened.
2250	  - The other flaw we could complain about is the Sample. Our author is promising every parent that if their child uses VIVVY, they can expect the same thing that happened when Amy, Matt, and Evelyn used it.
2251	  - What if our kids are different from Amy, Matt, and Evelyn?!
2252	  - The argument's sample size is crazy small. Because 3 users went on to be successful university students we should believe that every user will have the same experience?

2362	We shouldn't be 100% sure, but trusting Gloria is way safer than trusting the Senator, who was more external to the thing being discussed (unless the Senator is somehow part of the land-reclamation project, but we weren't told that).
2363	(C) This one is just like (A) and (B). It does have Source 2 contradicting something that Source 1 said or implied, and it unconditionally sides with Source 2.
2364	
2365	But again it lacks theInappropriate Expertpart of the original argument. It sounds like Dr. Treviso is just the right kind of expert you'd trust to judge whether someone is physiologically capable of holding their breath for 30 mins.
2366	(D) This has Source 2 contradicting Source 1, but Source 2 isalways rightabout the thing he's talking about (time).
2367	
2368	Furthermore, the last couple ideas here get into a different flaw:Necessary vs. Sufficientconfusion.
2369	
2370	If Robert is right, Evelyn couldn't hear late news. According to Evelyn, she didn't hear the late news. (that establishes the outcome, but we're not allowed to derive the trigger from that).
2371	
2372	Yet, the author does go and conclude that since the outcome was true, the trigger must be true; Robert must be right, which means Evelyn was wrong.
2373	(E) Again we have Source 2 contradicting Source 1, but this time Source 2 is anInappropriate Expert(just like in the original), whereas source 1 actually seems like a more knowledgeable party.
2374	
2375	Moira actually observed the end of the race, whereas Lomas is just an expert on how bikes get made. If the issue at hand is "did Lee finish ahead of Adams?", it seems like Source 1 is the more trustworthy source.

2378	=================== PT116-S3-Q22 | diff 3 | key D | score 16.5 | features: Famous Flaws, Explain Curious Fact
2379	STIMULUS: Taking advanced mathematics courses should increase a student's grade point average, for, as numerous studies have shown, students who have taken one or more advanced mathematics courses are far more likely to have high grade point averages than students who have not taken such courses.
2380	STEM: The flawed pattern of reasoning in the argument above is most similar to that in which one of the following?
2381	(A) [1% | Weak Match] Fur color is in large measure hereditary, for, as many studies have shown, black cats are more likely than others to have black kittens, and orange cats are more likely to have orange kittens.
2382	(B) [2% | Weak Evidence Match] Water can cause intoxication. After all, imbibing scotch and water, whiskey and water, bourbon and water, gin and water, and vodka and water all cause intoxication.
2383	(C) [6% | Weak Match] Eating a diet consisting primarily of fats and carbohydrates may cause weight gain in some people. Studies have shown that many overweight people eat such diets.
2384	(D) [73% | Correct] Buying running shoes should increase the frequency with which a person exercises, since those who buy two or more pairs of running shoes each year tend to exercise more often than those who buy at most one pair.
2385	(E) [19% | Not as Good as (D)] Reading to children at an early age should inspire them to read on their own later, since studies have shown that children who have not been read to are less likely to develop an interest in reading than children who have been read to.
2386	--- BREAKDOWN
2387	## Conclusion
2388	Taking advanced math should raise your GPA.
2389	## Evidence
2390	Numerous studies have shown that students who take at least one advanced math class are more likely than those who don't to have high GPA's.
2391	## Evaluation
2392	This is a classic move from **Correlation to Causality**. Whenever we see a correlation formulated like, "people who are X are more likely than those who aren't to be Y", we can expect the author to overconfidently assume/conclude that X causes Y, or that Y causes X.
2393	We are supposed to think about alternate explanations for this correlation (such as **Reverse Causality** or **Third Factor** ). It's possible that high GPA is a prerequisite to get into advanced math classes (reverse causality). It's possible that being really smart, or having a tutor, or having involved parents is the third causal factor that accounts for both high GPA and taking advanced math.
2394	## Goal
2395	We need an argument that presents a correlation and then concludes that one thing causes the other, but seems vulnerable to objections of reverse causality or third factor.
2396	--- CHOICE NOTES
2397	(A) This concludes that "genes cause fur color" (hereditary = genetic). Is there a correlation in the evidence that says "cats who had these genes were more likely than those who didn't to have a certain color fur"?
2398	
2399	No, not quite. Talking about a cat's offspring certainly conveys the idea of shared genes, but this isn't the same as presenting a correlation between supposed cause (genes) and supposed effect (fur color).
2400	
2401	Also, this correlation wouldn't be vulnerable to speculation of reverse causality (we wouldn't be able to argue that fur color caused the genes).
2402	(B) The conclusion is causal, although it's less strongly worded than the original. "X should have an effect on Y" seems stronger than saying "X can have an effect of Y".
2403	
2404	The evidence is not a correlation between X and Y, though. That would have sounded like, "People who drink water are more likely than those who don't to be intoxicated".
2405	
2406	But, this definitely deserves points for being one of the funniest LSAT answer choices we'll come across.
2407	(C) The conclusion is causal but more weakly worded than the original (X may cause Y vs. X should cause Y).
2408	
2409	And the evidence isn't quite the same type of correlation. We would want to hear something more like, "People whose diet is mainly fats and carbs are more likely than people without such a diet to have gained weight".
2410	
2411	This just says "many obese people eat such diets". Cool, but many non-obese people also each such diets. A correlation reveals a clear asymmetry because of how it's phrased.
2412	
2413	Many people who don't have high GPAs take advanced math, but the correlation is saying "yes, but you're more likely to have a high GPA if you take advanced math".
2414	(D) The conclusion is causal and has the same strength: buying running shoes should cause more exercise. The evidence is a correlation saying "people who are X are more likely than those who aren't to be Y".
2415	
2416	And this argument is definitely vulnerable to reverse causality objections (maybe exercising a bunch causes them to need to buy running shoes more often) or third factor objections (maybe wealthy people have more means to buy new running shoes and more disposable time to exercise).
2417	(E) This is a frustratingly close match, but not as close as (D). This does have a causal conclusion of the same strength as the original.
2418	
2419	We want the evidence to say "kids who have been read to are more likely than those who don't to read on their own later". The evidence basically does say that. It's presented in inverted format, but it's the same idea: "kids who haven't been read to are less likely".
2420	
2421	That slight difference would already award the victory of "closest match" to (D), but the other salient difference is that there's no way to argue reverse causality with this one, since it already has a chronology built in.
2422	
2423	In the original, we could object, "maybe they had a high GPA before they started doing advanced math". We (D), we can object, "maybe they started exercising more first, and then they needed more running shoes".
2424	
2425	But with this one, we can't say, "maybe they started reading on their own later in life before they were read to as a child".
2426
